[OUTPUT] (turn 12):
Mixed-integer linear programming

    Solves problems of the following form:

    .. math::

        \min_x \ & c^T x \\
        \mbox{such that} \ & b_l \leq A x \leq b_u,\\
        & l \leq x \leq u, \\
        & x_i \in \mathbb{Z}, i \in X_i

    where :math:`x` is a vector of decision variables;
    :math:`c`, :math:`b_l`, :math:`b_u`, :math:`l`, and :math:`u` are vectors;
    :math:`A` is a matrix, and :math:`X_i` is the set of indices of
    decision variables that must be integral. (In this context, a
    variable that can assume only integer values is said to be "integral";
    it has an "integrality" constraint.)

    Alternatively, that's:

    minimize::

        c @ x

    such that::

        b_l <= A @ x <= b_u
        l <= x <= u
        Specified elements of x must be integers

    By default, ``l = 0`` and ``u = np.inf`` unless specified with
    ``bounds``.

    Parameters
    ----------
    c : 1D dense array_like
        The coefficients of the linear objective function to be minimized.
        `c` is converted to a double precision array before the problem is
        solved.
    integrality : 1D dense array_like, optional
        Indicates the type of integrality constraint on each decision variable.

        ``0`` : Continuous variable; no integrality constraint.

        ``1`` : Integer variable; decision variable must be an integer
        within `bounds`.

        ``2`` : Semi-continuous variable; decision variable must be within
        `bounds` or take value ``0``.
... (205 lines total)

[CODE] (turn 13):
def min_moves_ilp(n):
    num_m = n-1
    num_t = n
    num_b = n
    total = num_m + num_t + num_b
    c = np.zeros(total)
    c[:num_m] = 1.0  # minimize sum of m_i
    
    # bounds: default non-negative, but need upper bound for b_i = 1
    lower = np.zeros(total)
    upper = np.full(total, np.inf)
    # set upper bound for b_i
    for i in range(num_b):
        idx = num_m + num_t + i
        upper[idx] = 1.0
    
    # Equality constraints for each vertex
    A_eq = np.zeros((n, total))
    b_eq = np.zeros(n)
    for i in range(n):
        # degree contributions
        if i > 0:
            A_eq[i, i-1] = 1  # m_{i-1}
        if i < n-1:
            A_eq[i, i] = 1    # m_i
        # -4*t_i
        A_eq[i, num_m + i] = -4
        # + b_i
        A_eq[i, num_m + num_t + i] = 1
        # RHS = r_i
        r_i = 2 if (i % 2 == 0) else 0  # i even => vertex odd => r=2
        b_eq[i] = r_i
    # Inequality sum b_i <= 2
    A_ub = np.zeros((1, total))
    A_ub[0, num_m+num_t:] = 1
    b_ub = np.array([2.0])
    
    from scipy.optimize import LinearConstraint, milp, Bounds
    constraints = []
    constraints.append(LinearConstraint(A_eq, b_eq, b_eq))
    constraints.append(LinearConstraint(A_ub, -np.inf, b_ub))
    
    bounds = Bounds(lower, upper)
    integrality = np.ones(total, dtype=int)
    res = milp(c=c, constraints=constraints, bounds=bounds, integrality=integrality, options={'presolve': True, 'disp': False})
    if not res.success:
        print("MILP failed:", res.message)
        return None
    m_vals = res.x[:num_m]
    total_m = int(round(m_vals.sum()))
    moves = total_m + 1
    return moves

for n in range(3,21,2):
    print(n, min_moves_ilp(n))

def min_moves_ilp(n):
    num_m = n-1
    num_t = n
    num_b = n
    total = num_m + num_t + num_b
    c = np.zeros(total)

    # set upper bound for b_i
    for i in range(num_b):

        upper[idx] = 1.0
    
    # Equality constraints for each vertex
    A_eq = np.zeros((n, total))
    b_eq = np.zeros(n)
    for i in range(n):

        if i > 0:

        if i < n-1:

        # -4*t_i

        # + b_i
        A_eq[i, num_m + num_t + i] = 1

    return moves

[CODE] (turn 14):
def solve_ilp_solution(n):
    num_m = n-1
    num_t = n
    num_b = n
    total = num_m + num_t + num_b
    c = np.zeros(total)
    c[:num_m] = 1.0
    lower = np.zeros(total)
    upper = np.full(total, np.inf)
    for i in range(num_b):
        upper[num_m+num_t+i] = 1.0
    A_eq = np.zeros((n,total))
    b_eq = np.zeros(n)
    for i in range(n):
        if i>0:
            A_eq[i,i-1]=1
        if i<n-1:
            A_eq[i,i]=1
        A_eq[i,num_m+i] = -4
        A_eq[i,num_m+num_t+i]=1
        r_i = 2 if (i%2==0) else 0
        b_eq[i]=r_i
    A_ub = np.zeros((1,total))
    A_ub[0,num_m+num_t:]=1
    b_ub=np.array([2.0])
    from scipy.optimize import LinearConstraint, milp, Bounds
    constraints=[LinearConstraint(A_eq,b_eq,b_eq), LinearConstraint(A_ub,-np.inf,b_ub)]
    bounds=Bounds(lower,upper)
    integrality=np.ones(total,dtype=int)
    res=milp(c=c,constraints=constraints,bounds=bounds,integrality=integrality,options={'presolve':True,'disp':False})
    if not res.success:
        print("fail")
        return None
    m_vals=res.x[:num_m]
    t_vals=res.x[num_m:num_m+num_t]
    b_vals=res.x[num_m+num_t:]
    return m_vals, t_vals, b_vals

[CODE] (turn 14):
def solve_ilp_solution(n):
    num_m = n-1
    num_t = n
    num_b = n
    total = num_m + num_t + num_b
    c = np.zeros(total)
    c[:num_m] = 1.0
    lower = np.zeros(total)
    upper = np.full(total, np.inf)
    for i in range(num_b):
        upper[num_m+num_t+i] = 1.0
    A_eq = np.zeros((n,total))
    b_eq = np.zeros(n)
    for i in range(n):
        if i>0:
            A_eq[i,i-1]=1
        if i<n-1:
            A_eq[i,i]=1
        A_eq[i,num_m+i] = -4
        A_eq[i,num_m+num_t+i]=1
        r_i = 2 if (i%2==0) else 0
        b_eq[i]=r_i
    A_ub = np.zeros((1,total))
    A_ub[0,num_m+num_t:]=1
    b_ub=np.array([2.0])
    from scipy.optimize import LinearConstraint, milp, Bounds
    constraints=[LinearConstraint(A_eq,b_eq,b_eq), LinearConstraint(A_ub,-np.inf,b_ub)]
    bounds=Bounds(lower,upper)
    integrality=np.ones(total,dtype=int)
    res=milp(c=c,constraints=constraints,bounds=bounds,integrality=integrality,options={'presolve':True,'disp':False})
    if not res.success:
        print("fail")
        return None
    m_vals=res.x[:num_m]
    t_vals=res.x[num_m:num_m+num_t]
    b_vals=res.x[num_m+num_t:]
    return m_vals, t_vals, b_vals

m_vals, t_vals, b_vals = solve_ilp_solution(7)
print("m:", m_vals)
print("b:", b_vals)

[OUTPUT] (turn 14):
m: [2. 2. 0. 0. 2. 1.]
b: [-0. -0.  0.  0.  0.  1.  1.]

[CODE] (turn 15):
def compute_degrees(m):
    n = len(m)+1
    d = [0]*n
    for i in range(n):
        if i>0:
            d[i] += m[i-1]
        if i<n-1:
            d[i] += m[i]
    return d

d = compute_degrees(m_vals)
print(d)

def check_solution(m,b):
    n = len(m)+1
    d = compute_degrees(m)
    ok=True
    for i in range(1,n+1):
        p = 1 if i%2==1 else 0
        r = 2 if p==1 else 0
        if b[i-1]==1:
            needed = (r - 1) % 4
        else:
            needed = r % 4
        if d[i-1] % 4 != needed:
            print(f"vertex {i} degree {d[i-1]} needed {needed}")
            ok=False
    print("ok", ok)
    return ok

check_solution(m_vals, b_vals)

[CODE] (turn 17):
def min_moves_bfs(n, max_steps=100):
    start_bits = tuple(0 if i%2==0 else 1 for i in range(n))
    target_bits = tuple([1]*n)
    from collections import deque
    q = deque()
    q.append((start_bits, None, 0))
    visited = set()
    visited.add((start_bits, None))
    while q:
        bits, pos, dist = q.popleft()
        if bits == target_bits:
            return dist
        if dist >= max_steps:
            continue
        if pos is None:
            # first move any coin
            for i in range(n):
                newbits = list(bits)
                newbits[i] ^= 1
                state = (tuple(newbits), i)
                if state not in visited:
                    visited.add(state)
                    q.append((state[0], state[1], dist+1))
        else:
            for i in (pos-1, pos+1):
                if 0 <= i < n:
                    newbits = list(bits)
                    newbits[i] ^= 1
                    state = (tuple(newbits), i)
                    if state not in visited:
                        visited.add(state)
                        q.append((state[0], state[1], dist+1))
    return None

print(min_moves_bfs(7, max_steps=20))

    target_bits = tuple([1]*n)
    from collections import deque
    q = deque()

    visited = set()
    visited.add((start_bits, None))

        if bits == target_bits:
            return dist
        if dist >= max_steps:
            continue
        if pos is None:
            # first move any coin
            for i in range(n):
                newbits = list(bits)
                newbits[i] ^= 1
                state = (tuple(newbits), i)
                if state not in visited:
                    visited.add(state)
                    q.append((state[0], state[1], dist+1))
        else:
            for i in (pos-1, pos+1):
                if 0 <= i < n:
                    newbits = list(bits)
                    newbits[i] ^= 1
                    state = (tuple(newbits), i)
                    if state not in visited:
                        visited.add(state)
                        q.append((state[0], state[1], dist+1))
    return None

    return int(3*(n-1)//2 + 2*((n-3)//4) + 1)

[REASONING] (turn 21):
n ≡ 1 mod 4, so minimal moves = 2n - 3 = 2*7873 - 3 = 15746 - 3 = 15743. Matches earlier computed.

Thus answer is 15743.

Now we need to provide reasoning: Show lower bound and construct strategy achieving that bound.

We can attempt to prove lower bound using invariant: each move flips a coin, changing number of tails. Initially there are (n+1)/2 tails. Goal zero tails. Each move can change number of tails by at most 1? Let's analyze: Flipping a coin toggles its state; if it's tails, becomes heads (decrease tails count by 1). If it's heads, becomes tails (increase tails count by 1). So net change in number of tails is either -1 or +1. So to reduce from (n+1)/2 to 0, we need at least (n+1)/2 moves that flip a tail to head, but we may also have moves that flip heads to tails (increase). So lower bound is at least (n+1)/2 moves. But that's far less than 2n.

Better invariant: Consider parity of sum of positions of tails? Or consider "potential" function like sum of distances between tails? Need stronger bound.

Observation: Since we must move adjacency, we need to travel across the row to flip tails. Starting anywhere, we need to reach each tail at least once. The distance traveled may be large.

We can think of each move as moving the "cursor" to adjacent coin and flipping it. The cursor moves along edges; total moves = number of flips = edges traversed + 1. So total edges traversed is moves -1.

Thus we need lower bound on total distance traveled (edges traversed). Since we need to visit each odd-indexed coin (tails) at least once, and we start anywhere, the minimal total edge traversals to visit all odd positions may be something like 2*(number of odd positions) - something.

But we also may need to revisit some even positions to move between tails.

Essentially we need to find minimal length of a walk that visits each odd vertex at least once (maybe more) on a path graph, starting anywhere.

This is similar to traveling salesman path problem on a line: minimal walk covering a set of required vertices (odd positions) can be found: you need to go from leftmost required to rightmost required, covering all in between. If you start at one end, you can go straight to the other end, covering all required vertices, length = distance between extremes = (rightmost - leftmost). But you also need to flip each required vertex exactly once (or odd times). Starting at leftmost required (position 1) and moving rightwards, you would flip each odd vertex when you encounter it. However you also flip even vertices (heads) as you pass them, turning them to tails (bad). But you could later flip them back. So extra moves needed to correct those.

Thus lower bound may involve needing to flip each even vertex at least twice (once when passing, once to revert). That yields extra cost.

Specifically, consider any walk that visits all odd vertices. As you move from left to right, you will inevitably step on even vertices. Each time you step onto an even vertex, you flip it (since each move flips visited coin). If you want final state heads, you must flip each even vertex an even number of times. Since you may pass over it multiple times, each pass flips it. So you need each even vertex to be visited an even number of times. Minimal even number is 0 (if you never visit it) but you may need to pass over it to reach odd vertices beyond. The minimal number of visits to each even vertex is either 0 or 2 (if you need to cross it). So each even vertex that lies between two odd vertices you must cross at least twice (once forward, once back) unless you start or end on one side.

Thus we can derive lower bound: For each even vertex that lies between two odd vertices that are both visited, you need at least 2 visits. For even vertices at ends (outside the range of odd vertices visited), you may need 0 or 1 visits depending on start/end.

Given the alternating pattern, the odd vertices are at positions 1,3,5,...,n. The even vertices are between them.

If we start at some odd vertex and end at another odd vertex, we will need to traverse the segment covering all odd vertices. The minimal walk that covers all odd vertices is to start at one odd end (maybe leftmost or rightmost) and walk straight to the other end, visiting each vertex sequentially. That walk visits each even vertex exactly once (since you step on them as you move). But even vertices need even number of visits, so each even vertex visited once is insufficient; you need to revisit them an extra time (i.e., you need to backtrack over each even vertex). That suggests extra moves equal number of even vertices (or maybe number of even vertices minus something). Let's compute.

If we start at leftmost odd (position 1) and walk to rightmost odd (position n), we traverse edges n-1 steps, visiting each vertex sequentially. Number of even vertices = (n-1)/2. Each even visited once. To make them visited twice, we need to add extra traversals that revisit each even vertex. The simplest is to backtrack over the whole path: after reaching rightmost odd, go back to leftmost odd, traversing edges again, adding another n-1 steps. This would make each even visited twice (once forward, once backward). Total edges traversed = 2*(n-1). Moves = 2*(n-1)+1 = 2n -1. But our minimal moves are 2n -3 or 2n -2, which is slightly less. So we can do better than full backtrack.

We can perhaps avoid revisiting the first and last even vertices because we can start or end at an even vertex? Wait start must be a flip, can be any coin. If we start at an even vertex (heads), we flip it to tails (bad), but later we can flip it again to heads. Starting at an even vertex may reduce need to revisit some evens.

Our derived minimal moves for n ≡ 1 mod 4 is 2n -3, which is 2 edges less than 2n -1. For n ≡ 3 mod 4, it's 2n -2, which is 1 edge less.

Thus we can achieve savings of 1 or 2 edges compared to full back-and-forth.

We need to construct optimal walk achieving these counts.

Observation: For n ≡ 1 mod 4 (n=4q+1), we can achieve moves = 2n -3 = 2*(4q+1)-3 = 8q -1. That's 2 edges less than 2n -1 (which would be 8q+1). So we saved 2 edges.

For n ≡ 3 mod 4 (n=4q+3), moves = 2n -2 = 8q+4, which is 1 edge less than 2n -1 = 8q+5.

Thus we need to design a walk that reduces backtracking.

Potential strategy: start at second coin (position 2) maybe? Let's attempt to construct algorithm for general n.

We can think of grouping coins in blocks of 4: pattern T H T H (positions 1-4). For each block, we can achieve flipping all tails with certain moves.

Maybe we can design a local pattern that flips a block of 4 coins using 6 moves (instead of 8). Let's test small block.

Consider block of 4 coins positions 1-4: T H T H. Goal: make all H. Starting anywhere within block, we can flip to achieve all H with minimal moves. Let's brute-force for n=4? But n must be odd. Let's consider block of 5 (positions 1-5) pattern T H T H T. Minimal moves for n=5 is 7 (as per formula). That's 2n -3 = 7. So for block of length 5 (mod 4 =1), we achieve 2n -3 moves.

For block of length 7 (mod 4 =3), minimal moves = 12 = 2n -2 = 12. So pattern holds.
